## Document 009: 115年9月海關進出口貿易初步統計 (2026-10-08)

115 年 9 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
9 月出口 872.2 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.9%，較上年
同月增 60.9%(按新臺幣計算增 68.2%)；第3 季出口 2,449.2 億美元，
較上年同季增 44.6%；累計 1 至 9 月 6,615.5 億美元，較上年同期增
46.2%(按新臺幣計算增 49.1%)。 
（二）進口 
9 月進口 635.9 億美元，亦創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.8%，較上
年同月增 51.7%(按新臺幣計算增 58.6%)；第 3 季進口 1,818.0 億美
元，較上年同季增 44.3%；累計 1 至 9 月 5,010.0 億美元，較上年同
期增 41.3%(按新臺幣計算增 43.8%)。 
（三）出入超 
9 月出超 236.3 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 113.4 億美元；
累計 1 至 9 月出超 1,605.5 億美元，較上年同期增 625.4 億美元。

二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於創新科技應用商機持續擴展，推升相關供應鏈出貨動能，加上
消費性電子新品備貨需求與產品價格上漲效應之挹注， 9 月出口較
上年同月增 330.0 億美元(+60.9%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 資通與視聽產品出口 391.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增
198.6 億美元(+1.0 倍)， 其中電腦及其附屬單元增157.4 億美元(+1.1
倍)、儲存媒體增 17.6 億美元(+2.2 倍)、交換器及路由器增 10.5
億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
2. 電子零組件出口 311.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增97.1
億美元(+45.3%)，其中積體電路增 93.9 億美元(+46.1%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+33.7%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.8 億美元(+32.0%)。 
3. 礦產品出口 22.6 億美元，較上年同月增 10.5 億美元(+86.4%)，其
中石油煉製品增 10.1 億美元(+99.0%)。 
4. 機械出口 27.4 億美元(歷年單月次高)，較上年同月增 6.4 億美元
(+30.7%)，其中生產半導體等機械增 2.7 億美元(+61.0%)、未列名
具特殊功能機械增 0.6 億美元(+73.6%)。 
5. 基本金屬及其製品出口 26.9 億美元，較上年同月增 5.0 億美元
(+22.9%)，其中銅及其製品增 2.5 億美元(+65.1%)、鋼鐵及其製品
增 1.4 億美元(+11.7%)。

（二）與上年同期比較 
1. 第 3 季出口較上年同季增 755.9 億美元(+44.6%)，以資通與視聽產
品增 367.6 億美元(+55.0%)、電子零組件增 308.4 億美元(+51.2%)
增加較多。 
2. 累計 1 至 9 月出口較上年同期增 2,089.8 億美元(+46.2%)，其中資
通與視聽產品增 1,222.2 億美元(+72.4%)、電子零組件增 726.6 億
美元(+45.7%)。

等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
隨 AI 產業鏈全球分工與出口引申需求擴增，電子零組件、資通產品
及半導體設備進口維持強勁，加上國際農工原料行情處於高檔，9
月進口較上年同月增 216.7 億美元(+51.7%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口440.5 億美元， 較上年同月增163.9億美元(+59.3%)，
其中電子零組件增 112.6 億美元(+82.5%)、電腦之零附件增 19.4
億美元(+1.5 倍)、礦產品增 17.1 億美元(+38.9%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 128.0 億美元， 較上年同月增 36.0 億美元(+39.1%)，
其中資通設備增 18.4 億美元(+75.0%)、半導體設備增 12.4 億美元
(+34.9%)，交通運輸設備減 1.4 億美元(-46.4%)。 
3. 消費品進口 62.1 億美元，較上年同月增 16.5 億美元(+36.0%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增 16.4 億美元(+5.3 倍)、筆電增 0.5 億
美元(+43.8%)，藥粧用品減 1.2 億美元(-16.6%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月對東協出口創新高，對美國、陸港為單月次高 
1. 對美國出口 274.6 億美元，較上年同月增 141.5 億美元(+1.1 倍)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 124.1 億美元(+1.3 倍)、 電子零組件增10.1
億美元(+1.3 倍)、機械增 2.2 億美元(+42.3%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 206.2 億美元，較上年同月增 47.8 億美元
(+30.2%)，其中電子零組件增 34.9 億美元(+30.5%)、資通與視聽
產品增 5.7 億美元(+39.0%)、機械增 1.9 億美元(+43.7%)。 
3. 對東協出口 188.8 億美元，較上年同月增 91.2 億美元(+93.4%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 49.7 億美元(+2.2 倍)、電子零組件增 32.0
億美元(+63.3%)、礦產品增 4.8 億美元(+1.4 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 45.3 億美元，較上年同月增 7.7 億美元(+20.6%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 5.7 億美元(+34.1%)、電子零組件增 2.2 億美
元(+56.6%)，基本金屬及其製品減 0.8 億美元(-20.5%)。 
5. 對日本出口 30.7 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+13.4%)，其
中電子零組件增 1.5 億美元(+11.0%)、基本金屬及其製品增 0.6 億
美元(+32.5%)、機械增 0.5 億美元(+29.1%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月對美國出口 2,077.8 億美元，較上年同期增 773.1 億美
元(+59.3%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,600.9 億美元，增 355.3 億美
元(+28.5%)， 對東協出口1,339.6 億美元 ， 增463.6 億美元(+52.9%)，
對歐洲出口 421.8 億美元 ， 增138.4 億美元(+48.8%)， 對日本出口277.7
億美元，增 58.3 億美元(+26.6%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月自中國大陸與香港進口 135.0 億美元 ， 較上年同月增50.1 億美元
(+59.1%)，自南韓進口 116.3 億美元，增52.6 億美元(+82.5%)，自東
協進口 106.0 億美元，增 52.6 億美元(+98.4%)，自日本進口 64.3 億
美元，增 17.7 億美元(+38.0%)，自歐洲進口 50.2 億美元，增 3.5 億
美元(+7.4%)，自美國進口 48.1 億美元，增 6.5 億美元(+15.5%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增77.6%、
自南韓增 65.8%、自中國大陸與香港增 42.8%、自日本增 25.7%、自
美國增 21.4%、自歐洲增 10.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）主要國家/地區今年 1-8 月外貿景氣普遍走升，以南韓(1-9 月)出口年
增 56.8%最為強勁，香港、新加坡亦分別年增 49.0%、39.8%；中國
大陸、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-7 月)、日本各增
9.7%、8.3%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-8 月以香港進口年增 50.5%較
突出，新加坡、中國大陸分別年增 37.3%、27.0%，南韓(1-9 月)、德
國(1-7 月)分別增 19.7%、10.0%，日本、美國各增 7.8%、4.2%。

### Page 7

7
圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）受惠於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及雲端服務等應用商機持續擴展，
加以步入傳統外銷旺季 、產品價格上漲效應，9 月出口 872.2 億美元，
年增 60.9%，連續 35 個月正成長；進口635.9 億美元，年增51.7%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口引申需求擴張，及國際原物料行情居
高；出、進口值及出超 236.3 億美元齊創單月新高。累計 1 至 9 月出
口年增 46.2%、進口增 41.3%。
（二）9 月主要貨品出口普遍較上年同月成長，以資通與視聽產品增 1.0 倍
最佳，包括顯示卡、伺服器、儲存媒體、 交換器及路由器等出口均
呈倍數增加 ；其次為礦產品增 86.4%，主因全球油品供應偏緊支撐價
格維持高檔；電子零組件隨 AI 應用及消費性電子新品備貨需求升溫，
增 45.3%，機械、電機產品亦各增 3 成上下。累計 1 至 9 月資通與視
聽產品、電子零組件出口併計增 59.5%，占總出口比重 79.0%。

（三）主要市場方面，9 月對美國、東協出口值分創歷年單月次高、新高，
年增率各為 1.1 倍及 93.4%，合計對總出口增加貢獻達 7 成；對中國
大陸與香港、歐洲、日本出口各年增 30.2%、20.6%、13.4%。累計 1
至 9 月對五大市場出口值皆為歷年同期最高，其中對美國、東協均
提前刷新全年紀錄 ， 分別年增59.3%、52.9%，對歐洲出口年增48.8%，
對陸港、日本各增 28.5%、26.6%。
（四）展望未來，全球貿易政策及地緣政治情勢變化等，仍增添總體 經濟
不確定性，惟隨人工智慧應用 持續深化，國際雲端服務業者 及各國
加速 AI 基礎建設投資，帶動半導體、伺服器等相關供應鏈需求擴增，
加以科技新品備貨、中國大陸及歐美年底採購旺季將屆， 均有利挹
注我國外銷動能，第 4 季出口可望延續成長態勢。

世界経済：AI関連需要の広がりが世界景気をけん引。企業の景況感は堅調に推移
 製造業は節目50を上回る良好な水準を維持。非製造業は金融・消費関連を中心に加速
― 製造業はAI関連投資がけん引し、防衛支出の拡大も寄与。一方、中東情勢を受けた在庫積み増しの寄与は縮小傾向
― 非製造業は金融サービスやAI関連需要がけん引。エネルギー高で落ち込んでいた観光・娯楽も回復
 国・地域別では、米国では非製造業が大幅に改善。日本やユーロ圏では製造業を中心に改善

― 日本ではAI・半導体需要と外需拡大がけん引し、ユーロ圏は電気・電子部品を含む中間財の生産が好調。AI投資ブー
ムの広がりがうかがわれる
1
製造業の景況感（購買担当者景気指数） 非製造業の景況感（購買担当者景気指数）
（注） 日米欧：S&P Global、中：国家統計局。購買担当者景気指数：企業に対するアン
ケート調査結果から作成した指数。新規受注や生産高、雇用、出荷遅延などの指数
に一定のウエイトを掛けて算出。50が景気拡大・縮小の分岐点
（出所）S&P Global、中国国家統計局より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
（注） 日米欧：S&P Global、中：国家統計局。購買担当者景気指数（非製造業）：企業に対

### Page 3

金融市場概況：原油高を背景に日米金利が上昇
 日米株価は、9月下旬にハイテク株を中心に上昇
― 9月上旬は、日米株価とも軟調。原油高を背景とした日米金利上昇が重荷に
― 下旬に入ると、米AI関連株が買い戻されたことからナスダックが上昇に転じ、日経平均もつれ高の展開に
 ドル円は一時円高が進展したものの、日米金利差が拡大し、再度150円台後半まで円安が進む「往って来い」の展開に
― 日本・米国とも利上げを実施。先行きも利上げ継続見込みだが、米金利上昇幅がより大きく、日米金利差は拡大
― 日銀は物価上振れリスクに対応し、利上げペースを加速へ。次回利上げは26年12月会合と予想

米国：個人消費は一時的に減速も、サービス中心に底堅さ維持
 7月実質個人消費支出は前月比＋0.0％と減速も、一時要因による下押しが大きく、消費の基調は崩れていないと評価
― 例年より前倒しで6月に実施された大手通販セールの反動から、財消費が減少
― 一方、そうした影響を受けづらいサービス消費については強さを維持。高齢化を背景に医療支出が拡大したほか、W杯
開催等を受け、娯楽サービス、外食・宿泊、輸送サービスなどの選択的支出も増加
 小売販売の高頻度データは、足元で伸びが再び高まっており、7月の減速が一時的であったことを示唆

米国：所得減速下でも株高による資産効果が消費を下支え
 消費が底堅い背景に、富裕層を中心とした株価上昇による資産効果の持続
― 株高を受けてインフレ下でも家計の実質金融資産は着実に増加。可処分所得対比でも高水準を維持。実質所得が弱含
む中でも、金融資産の増加が家計の購買力を下支え
 先行きは物価鈍化に伴う実質所得の改善も消費の追い風に
― 実質可処分所得は前年比＋0.5％と伸び悩みが継続。先行きは物価鈍化を受けて実質所得も伸び幅を徐々に持ち直す

### Page 6

米国：雇用は予想外の大幅増。一時的要因の影響を受けつつも労働市場の底堅さを確認
 8月の非農業部門雇用者数は前月差＋16.2万人と市場予想（同＋5.6万人）を大幅に上回る結果
― 過去2カ月も合わせて5.5万人の上方修正。速報段階で減少した7月もプラス転化
― 娯楽・宿泊（同＋6.2万人）や教育・医療（同＋2.8万人）を中心に民間就業者が伸びを高めたほか、政府部門も同＋3.5
万人増と押し上げに寄与
― 政府部門では、バックトゥスクールに伴い教員、スクールバス運転士、カフェテリアスタッフなどの雇用が増加（例年みら
れる季節的な動きであるが、統計上の調整にゆがみが生じている可能性）

（年初来変動幅、万人）
（年/月）
失業率は低位で横ばい
低失業率の一因に「少ない供給」
米国：失業率は低位安定。厳格化する移民政策が労働供給を一段と下押し
 失業率は4.1％と前月からほぼ横ばい。引き続きFOMC参加者が見込む長期均衡水準（Longer run）を下回る低水準
 低失業率の一因に労働参加率の低下。シニア層の退職増加に加え、移民政策の厳格化の影響も大
― 米移民・関税執行局（ICE）の摘発増など移民取り締まりが強化されるなか、メキシコ出身者などの労働人口は減少傾向
― 足元ではハイチなど複数国の一時保護資格（TPS、※）が相次ぎ終了。今後もエルサルバドルなどで有効期限を迎え、7

月以降の累計で最大100万人程度がTPSに基づく就労資格を失う可能性。労働供給を更に押し下げる要因に
（※）紛争や災害等により安全な帰国が困難な特定国の出身者に対し、一時的な在留・就労を認める制度
6
（出所）米労働省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
失業率 移民の出身国別労働人口とTPS終了の対象国・規模
（注）TPS保有者数は各国について入手可能な最新値。ベネズエラは2023年指定分。9月以降のTPS終了
時期は現時点での有効期限であり、今後の延長・終了決定等により変更される可能性がある
（出所）米労働省、米移民評議会、米国土安全保障省、米議会調査局より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

米国：エネルギー高・関税でディスインフレは足踏み、期待インフレも高止まり
 エネルギー高や関税が重石となり、インフレ率の低下は足踏み
―8月のCPIが前年比＋3.4％と横ばいにとどまるなか、コアCPIも同＋2.4％（7月同＋2.5％）と減速は緩やか。サービス価
格は原油高を背景に航空運賃などが上昇、財価格には関税の影響が残存
 消費者の期待インフレ率は3％台で高止まり、2％目標への収束はなお見通しづらい状況
―FRB高官からは、5年以上に及ぶ目標超過で高インフレが定着するリスクを警戒する声も

（年）
欧州：ドイツでは防衛・インフラ投資の効果が一部発現。恩恵は年後半以降一段と顕在化
9
 財政拡張を支えに、ドイツでは防衛・インフラ関連業種の生産・受注が拡大
― 生産水準が急速に高まる武器・弾薬の他、足元では鉄道車両の生産も拡大
― 公共部門によるインフラ投資の受注残は直近2四半期で＋10％超の増加
 今後の財政支出余地を踏まえれば、当該業種への恩恵は年後半以降、一段と顕在化する見込み
―6月末時点の執行額は既に2025年通年の約8割に達するも、年間予算に対する執行率は4割弱にとどまる

― 懸念材料は再び上昇し始めた原油価格。高インフレが再燃すれば、個人消費の回復に水を差す懸念

日本：日銀追加利上げは最短インターバル。今後も日銀は3カ月に1回程度のペースで利上げへ
17
 日銀は9月会合で利上げを決定（反対2名）。利上げは6月以来3カ月ぶりで、今次局面では最も短いインターバル
―9月会合後の会見で植田総裁は、物価押し上げから物価上振れ抑制へと「政策の局面が変化した」と説明
― 政策金利は1.25％と、日銀による中立金利推計値のレンジ下限を上回るが、金融環境は利上げ後も緩和的と評価
 今後も日銀は3カ月に1回程度のペースで利上げを続ける可能性大
― 大幅利上げや連続利上げについては物価上振れリスクが大きい場合の選択肢と説明。一方、急激な政策変更を回避す

る観点から、先手を打って利上げを実施していく姿勢
物価上振れリスクへの対応から
利上げ間隔が短期化
政策金利とブレークイーブン・インフレ
（注） ブレークイーブン・インフレは基調的物価上昇率の一面を捉えていると考えられるが、
必ずしも基調的物価上昇率そのものを示す指標ではない点に留意
（出所）日本銀行、LSEGより、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成 （出所） 日本銀行より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
日銀9月会合のポイント（声明文・総裁会見）
声明文
• 企業の賃金・価格設定行動の積極化や中長期的な予想物価上昇
率の上昇などを踏まえると、2％物価目標を上振れるリスクがある
• 企業間取引における価格上昇圧力が消費者段階にも波及し始め
ているほか、賃金上昇の販売価格への転嫁の動きも続いている
• 政策金利の変更後も、緩和的な金融環境は維持される
会見での注目発言
• 基調的物価上昇率が2％に接近し、政策局面が変化。物価上振れ
を回避する観点が重要に
• 原油価格の上昇やそこから波及した物価上昇圧力、AI関連需要

や円安進行が幅広い品目の物価上昇圧力に繋がる可能性
• 基調的物価の2％定着を確認するのにはしばらく時間がかかる
• 金融緩和の度合いは利上げにより少しずつ縮まっているが、銀行
貸し出しや資産市場は依然として緩和的
• 急激な利上げで金融環境が大きく変化することや、それによる経
済へのマイナス影響を回避する観点は重要
• 大幅利上げに追い込まれる可能性を減らすように政策を運営する

米国債券市場：インフレ懸念と追加利上げ観測を背景に利回りは上昇
 9月の米10年国債利回りは、インフレ懸念の高まりから5.2％まで上昇
― 月前半は、雇用統計の上振れや原油高、物価指標の高止まりから、長期金利は5％の大台に
― 中旬は、FOMCが25bpの利上げを決定。追加利上げの可能性も示唆され、長期金利は5％前後で高止まり
― 月後半は、堅調な景況感やFRB高官のタカ派発言を背景に、長期金利は5.2％台まで上昇
 今後は、ホルムズ海峡を巡る中東情勢の動向が鍵に。原油高が続けば、複数回の追加利上げにも現実味
― トランプ大統領がイランの提示した停戦案を拒否するなど、中東情勢の先行きは依然として不透明

国内債券・為替：長期金利は一時3.1％超え。ドル円は往って来いで円安継続へ
 長期金利は上昇。一時3.115％と1996年8月ぶりの水準に
― 日銀9月会合前後で利上げ前倒しを織り込む動き。米FRBの追加利上げ観測も、円安進行を通じて金利上昇の要因に
― 先行きも原油価格や米金利の高止まりが見込まれるもと、日本の長期金利も3％超での推移が続く可能性
 9月のドル円は往って来い。先行きも、150円台を中心とした円安水準が続く見込み
― 9月前半は、ベッセント財務長官の円安警戒発言や日銀利上げ観測の高まりを受け、一時152円台後半まで円高に
― もっとも、円高基調は定着せず。9月後半にかけては米利上げ観測の高まりを背景に150円台後半まで再度円安が進行

内外株式：原油高が重石も、AI関連需要を支えに日米株はともに底堅く推移
 日米株は、中東情勢の緊迫化に伴う原油高が重石となるも、AI・半導体関連需要を支えに底堅く推移
― 日経平均は月半ばの調整後、半導体関連株がけん引し上昇。S&P500も最高値圏を維持
―WTI 原油価格は、サウジアラビアの主要パイプライン停止等から一時100ドル超へ急伸。パイプラインの部分的な稼働
再開を受け月半ば以降は反落も、インフレ懸念に伴う金利上昇が株価の重石に
 日経VIは緩やかに低下、米VIXも低位横ばい圏。他方で原油・金利動向を巡る振れやすさには引き続き留意

― 中東情勢を受けた変動は一時的に留まった一方、米・イラン間の協議停滞など原油高が株価を圧迫する構図は残存

營收走勢圖

研究員/陳晏平 TINA.YP.CHEN@uni-psg.com

1H26 主要認列案源為中和 K Tower Y09 商辦案、中和心禾匯住宅
案、台北信義豪宅案持續去化與遞延交屋，1H26 累積營收為 132 億
元 ， 成長31%YOY， 且在整體毛利率較2025 年同期些微提升下 ，1H26
累積稅後淨利為 11.1 億元，成長 61%YOY，EPS 為 1.87 元。展望
2H26，兩個台中新完工建案(合計總銷金額 83 億元)遞延至 4Q26 才
正式推出銷售，且台中市地區為 2026 年以來銷售速度較慢的區域，
預期此兩個台中建案在 2026 年認列案量較少，主要將貢獻在 2027
年。所幸，近幾個月財富效果開始顯現，進而有提升居住品質、資
產配置需求，台北市信義豪宅案 1H26 已認列逾 10 億元，銷售優於
預期 。 因此 ， 研究部預估2026 年合併營收為 268 億元 ，成長 17%YOY，
稅後淨利 20 億元，成長 27%YOY，EPS 為 3.24 元，獲利將自谷底回
升；並預估 2027 年合併營收為 301 億元，成長 12%YOY，稅後淨利
為 24 億元，成長 21%YOY，EPS 為 3.92 元，營運將呈現穩增。投資
建議方面，雖目前房市相關政策已不再緊縮，並朝向逐步鬆綁，加
上高科技產業景氣成長強勁、股市上漲帶來財富效果，使得房市氣
氛將逐漸走出谷底，但因研究部預期冠德 2026~2027 年獲利皆將落
在 3~4 元之譜，未明顯跳增，近兩年現金殖利率亦約當 4~5%，未明
顯高於同業，故維持給予0.5xNAV，目標價40 元，潛在上漲空間有
限，重申中立建議。

重點摘要： 
1. 1H26認列案源主要為帳上成屋持續去化，累積稅後 EPS為1.87
元： 
受惠:(1) 中和K Tower Y09( 原中和秀朗橋捷運聯開商辦案，
總銷金額31億元，銷售率30%)新完工建案開始交屋；(2) 中和
心禾匯(原中和LG08捷運聯開住宅案，總銷金額16億元，銷售
率80%)、 台北市冠德信義豪宅案持續去化與遞延交屋入帳 ；(3)
轉投資根基營造合併營收貢獻大增75%QOQ、52%YOY至61億元，
進而帶動2Q26單季合併 營收明顯成長72%QOQ、57%YOY至84億
元。另在營造子公司有個案結算以及高毛利率冠德信義豪宅案
認列較多下 ， 毛利率亦有所提升，2Q26單季毛利率上揚至28%，
季增4個百分點、年增5個百分點，再扣除費用、所得稅負、根
基少數股權影響，2Q26單季稅後淨利為8億元(歸屬母公司)，
跳增160%QOQ、224%YOY，單季EPS為1.35元。

合計1H26累積營收為132億元，成長31%YOY，且在整體毛利率
較2025年同期些微提升下，1H26累積稅後淨利為11.1億元，成
長61%YOY，EPS為1.87元。

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

2. 兩個台中新完工建案遞延至4Q26才正式推出銷售， 預估2026年
稅後EPS將自谷底回升至3.24元： 
展望2H26，除帳上成屋案持續去化外，冠德 規劃10~11月將陸
續推出台中裕毛屋住宅案(總銷金額67億元)、 台中G5捷運聯開
商辦案(總銷金額16億元)、台北市萬華直興住宅 案(總銷金額
36億元)，合計新推出案量共119億元。其中，台中裕毛屋住宅
案已於2026年6月取得使照，並有先行潛銷，預計2026年10月
正式推出銷售 ；台中G5捷運聯開商辦案亦已取得使照，計畫
2026年11月開始銷售 ，此兩案皆 將視銷售成績在2H26交屋入
帳。

考量:(1) 台中為2026年以來房屋銷售較慢的區域，且台中裕
毛屋住宅案、台中G5捷運聯開商辦案皆延至4Q26才開始銷售，
預期2026年認列比例將較原先預期少，推估將分別認列 20%、
10%案量；(2) 中和心禾匯、中和K Tower Y09可於2026年順利
銷售並完成交屋25%、1/3案量；(3) 財富效果顯現，進而有提
升居住品質、資產配置需求，預期台北市冠德信義豪宅案可去
化20億元金額，較2025年認列金額倍增。再加計營造、百貨事
業營收貢獻 ， 預估2026年合併營收為268億元 ，成長17%YOY，稅
後淨利20億元，成長27%YOY，EPS為3.24元，將自谷底回升。

3. 2027年將有一台北市住宅案完工 ，加計帳上成屋去化， 預估
2027年稅後EPS將穩增至3.92元： 
展望2027年，除持續銷售前述個案外，預期4Q27還將新推出南
港台電公辦都更住宅案，該案總銷金額達130億元，將視銷售
情況於2028年完工之時認列。而在建案認列方面，預期將有總
銷金額36億元的台北市萬華直興住宅案於3Q27完工 ， 研究部採
保守原則，推估此案在2027年將可順利去化並認列30%案量，
加計:(1)中和心禾匯、中和K Tower Y09、台中裕毛屋住宅案、
台中G5捷運聯開商辦案 ， 推估將分別去化 、 認列15%、1/3、60%、
60%案量 ；(2)台北市冠德信義豪宅案遞延交屋與持續去化20億
元；(3) 營造 、 百貨事業營收貢獻， 預估2027年合併營收為301
億元，成長12%YOY，稅後淨利為24億元，成長21%YOY，EPS為
3.92元，營運將呈現穩增。

4. 帳上土地庫存充足，估計每股資產價值為77元，目前股價評價
約0.5xNAV： 
在扣除2027年完工建案後，帳上土地庫存仍有2.2萬坪，換算
總銷金額為801億元 ， 未來推案無虞 。 就區域分布上 ， 以台北市
佔比最高，高達62%，其次為高雄市、台中市，佔比各為15%、
14%，另有板橋佔比9%。主要個案包含:板橋官邸公辦都更住宅
案、南港台電公辦都更住宅案、台北市行二行三公辦都更案、

### Page 3

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  3

台中G9-1捷運聯開商辦案、北士科合建住宅案、台北市兒福B2
公辦都更住宅案、板橋Y16捷運聯開商辦案、高雄專四五公辦
都更住商案、台北市通化安和公辦都更住宅案。其中，總銷金
額130億元的南港台電公辦都更住宅案 有望在4Q27推出，若順
銷，將成為2028年業績入帳來源。

除前述在建中建案、土地庫存外，冠德帳上還有2025年新完工
的總部大樓—台北市民權東路商辦案 ，總銷坪數1萬坪，其中
有2000餘坪為冠德自用，為提升商業價值，目前冠德傾向先行
出租，短期暫不會有出售計畫。而目前出租簽約率已達100%，
租客將在2026年陸續進駐 ， 估計一整年可貢獻4億元租金收入 。
整體而言，推估冠德每股資產價值為77元，目前股價位置約當
0.5xNAV。

### Page 4

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  4

5. 預期2027年現金殖利率將在4.4~4.9%區間水準： 
股利政策方面，為保留資本，以提高自有資金比例、降低營運
槓桿，近幾年在正常獲利情況下，現金股利配發率皆落在
35~41%區間水準 。 而因2025年獲利衰退較多 ，2026年配發1.616
元現金股利 ， 且無股票股利 ， 現金股利配發率提升至62%， 已於
2026/8/10除息，除息前現金殖利率4.7%。由於2026年獲利將
自谷底回升，研究部預期2027年現金股利將落在1.6~1.8元區
間水準，換算現金殖利率4.4~4.9%。

供 專 屬 之 投 資 建 議 。 文 中 所 載 資 訊 或 任 何 意 見 ， 不 構 成 任 何 買 賣 有 價 證 券 或 其 他 投

陳 述 或 保 證 。 本 投 資 報 告 載 述 意 見 進 行 更 改 與 撤 回 並 不 另 行 通 知 。 本 投 資 報 告 並 非

周建霖

光環(3234) 
買進, TP 240 (潛在報酬 32.23%)  
買進 
Price(10/02):181.5 
Target Price：240

在完成客戶 5,000 小時可靠度測試認證後，光環代工業務自 26H2
起重新貢獻營運， 隨稼動率提升及產品組合優化， 本業獲利已顯著
改善並順利轉虧為盈 。 為配合客戶後續產能規劃， 公司亦將斥資約
11 億元進行產線擴充，預期至2027 年底產能規模將達 2026 年底
的 3 倍。此外，針對具 Scale-up 應用潛力的 VCSEL 技術，光環
亦規劃推出 1060nm VCSEL 方案供客戶驗證，有望切入新一代
AI 光互連市場，進一步強化公司中長期競爭優勢。綜合以上，研
究部預估光環 2026 年 EPS 0.57 元，2027 年 EPS 6.77 元，目前
PE 為 26.5x，參考光通上游同業 PE 評價為 20-60x，考量光環本
業獲利將開始由虧轉盈且顯著成長，且也開始佈局 Scale-up 雷射
方案，因此給予 35xPER，目標價 240 元，建議買進。 
⚫ 公司基本資料 ⚫ 公司基本資料 
光環(3234)成立於 1997 年，主要產品為光通訊主動元件，包含雷
射晶粒切割、次模組封裝，2025 年晶粒及零組件佔 63%、光電轉
換件 28%、其他 9%。應用領域則為頻寬網路 10%、4G/5G 基地
台互聯 31%、雲端數據中心 22%、感測應用 26%、其他 11%，銷
售地區為台灣 60%、 中國8%、 歐洲及美洲17%、 亞洲及其他15%。

殖利率(%) - - ⚫ 營運概況 
取得客戶測試認證，代工業務重回貢獻 
光環 26Q2 營收 2.0 億元，Q+7.5%，Y+28.1%，毛利率 19.82%，
稅後淨利-1744 萬元，Q 持續虧損，Y 持續虧損，EPS -0.16 元。
在重新取得客戶 5000 小時的測試認證下，代工收入逐步重回貢獻
且產品組合優化下，使整體毛利率回升至雙位數以上，並在費用控
管得宜讓本業獲利虧損顯著收斂。

代工事業加速成長，本業由虧轉盈 
光環 8 月營收 1.1 億元，M+13.1%，Y+110.1%，7 月營收 9604
萬元，M+25.2%，Y+96.9%，7-8 月合計 2.0 億元，Y+103.7%。
代工事業逐月增加片數 ， 研究部認為在稼動率回升及產品組合優化
下，光環獲利將持續獲得顯著改善，其中7 月自結稅後淨利約 900
萬，EPS 0.08 元，反映出本業隨營收的放大而已轉虧為盈。綜合
以 上 ， 研 究 部 預 估 光 環26Q3 營收 3.2 億元， Q+63.9% ，
Y+113.2%，毛利率 33.77%，稅後淨利 4462 萬元，Q 虧轉盈，Y
虧轉盈，EPS 0.35 元。

產能積極布局，同時開發 1060nm VCSEL 自有產品 
光環今年以來已完成 16.5 億元現金增資(稀釋 EPS 約 11.8%)，並
公告將斥資約 11 億元進行產能擴充及設備擴置。研究部推估，此
次擴產主要係配合客戶 2027～2028 年產能規劃 ， 因而提前建置相
對應代工設備，預期至 2027 年底整體產能規模有望達 2026 年底
的 3 倍，且公司亦已著手規劃 2028 年後續擴產計畫。綜上所述，
預期光環代工業務將自 26H2 起加速成長，隨產品組合改善，本業
可望轉為穩定獲利，加以產能利用率提升帶動營運槓桿逐步顯現，
預期整體獲利增幅將優於營收成長。此外，光環深耕VCSEL 市場
多 年 ， 而 今 年 以 來 多 家 國 際 光 通 訊 及 雷 射 大 廠 亦 陸 續 提 出 以

VCSEL 作為 Scale-up 光互連光源的方案 。 相較SiPh 方案 ，VCSEL
具備高頻寬密度、低功耗、低延遲潛力及高可靠度等優勢，過往傳
輸 距 離 較 短 的 限 制 ， 在 以 機 櫃 內 及 機 櫃 間 短 距 離 互 連 為 主 的
Scale-up 應用中影響亦相對有限。研究部認為，若未來 Scale-up
架構朝 Wide-and-Slow 方向發展，VCSEL 將成為極具競爭力的技
術選項。光環憑藉既有技術與量產經驗，目前也開始佈局1060nm
的 VCSEL 方案，預計於年底推出樣品給客戶測試，未來有望成為
強化公司長期競爭優勢的自有產品。綜合以上，研究部預估光環
2026 年營收 11.0 億元 ，Y+60.7%， 毛利率28.29%， 稅後淨利7233
萬元 ，Y 虧轉盈 ，EPS 0.57 元；2027 年營收 28.8 億元 ，Y+162.0%，
毛利率 40.89%，稅後淨利 8.6 億元，Y+1083.9%，EPS 6.77 元。

結論 
在完成客戶 5,000 小時可靠度測試認證後，光環代工業務自 26H2
起重新貢獻營運，隨稼動率提升及產品組合優化，本業獲利已顯著
改善並順利轉虧為盈。為配合客戶後續產能規劃，公司亦將斥資約
11 億元進行產線擴充， 預期至2027 年底產能規模將達 2026 年底
的 3 倍。此外，針對具 Scale-up 應用潛力的 VCSEL 技術，光環
亦規劃推出 1060nm VCSEL 方案供客戶驗證 ， 有望切入新一代AI
光互連市場，進一步強化公司中長期競爭優勢。綜合以上，研究部

## Document 125: [000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에소음보다 방향성에 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에소음보다 방향성에 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

기업분석 2025.09.28 www.daishin.com

Issue & News 
SK하이닉스  
(000660) 
 소음보다 방향성에 
류형근

hyungkeun.ryu@daishin.com

투자의견 BUY  
매수, 유지 
6개월 목표주가 3,200,000 
유지  
현재주가 
(26.09.23) 
1,862,000 
반도체업종

◼ 최근 시장은 2가지 Risk를 제기. 다만, 과도한 우려는 기우. 
◼ 현 주가는 보다 강인해지는 Fundamental의 가치를 미반영한 수준. 
◼ 비중 확대 전략 유효. 목표주가와 매수의견 유지.

투자전략: 업황을 대변하지 못하고 있는 주가 
목표주가와 매수의견 유지. 현 주가는 업황의 강세를 충분히 반영하지 못하
고 있다고 생각. 1) 메모리반도체 사이클의 추가 확장 (구매 경쟁 심화에 따른 
단기 가격 상승 폭 확대, 기대치를 상회할 4Q26 DRAM 판매량), 2) 3Q26 실
적발표에서 보다 구체화될 주주환원 정책의 방향성에 보다 집중할 필요. 
시장에서 제기하는 Risk가 있다면, 2가지. 1) 주요 고객사향 HBM4 품질 이슈, 
2) Solidigm IPO와 해외 공장 투자에 따른 경계심리 확대가 그 대상. 당사는 
해당 Risk가 Fundamental을 저해할 것으로 보고 있지 않으며, 단기 노이즈보
다는 사이클 확장의 선순환에 보다 주목할 필요가 있다고 생각. 
1. HBM4 품질 이슈, 과도한 우려는 기우 
3Q26부터 HBM4 출하가 본격화될 것이라는 기존 입장을 유지. HBM4 비중
은 3Q26 전체 판매량의 40%, 2027년 연간 50% 중반까지 상승할 것. 
일각에서는 주요 고객사향 HBM4 품질 이슈로 사업 성과가 기대치를 하회할 
수 있다는 우려를 표출. 다만, 과도한 우려는 기우. 후공정 단에서 문제가 일
부 발생했을 수 있으나, 해결 불가능한 Risk라 보기 어렵고, 불량에 따른 배
상금을 논할 단계도 아니라는 판단. 
2. 해외 공장 투자, 실보다 득이 크다 
언론보도에 따르면, SK하이닉스는 해외 공장 투자와 Solidigm IPO를 추진 중. 
해당 결정이 현실화될 경우, 시장은 단기적으로 이를 부정적으로 받아들일 
가능성이 존재. 다만, 결과적으로, 실보다 득이 큰 선택이 될 것이라 생각. 
- 해외 투자가 현실화되더라도, 실질 생산 기여 시점은 2028년 이후일 가능
성이 높음. 미국의 건설 경기와 공장 구축의 세부 단계 등을 감안할 필요. 
- 해외 투자 결정이 관세 Risk를 축소시킬 수 있는 변화라는 점도 감안할 필
요. “불확실성 해소”는 Upcycle의 중반부에서 반도체 주식에 대한 좋은 투자
포인트가 되어왔고, 이번에도 다르지 않을 것이라는 판단.

## Document 166: Allianz_2026-10-09_Toasted, not burnt France s bond sell-off and the road to the 2027 election_99a3202d

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-09_Toasted, not burnt France s bond sell-off and the road to the 2027 election_99a3202d.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-09
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

In Summary 
• France is the hardest hit in the global bond sell-off, mostly due to its own fiscal and political 
weakness. Since the Hormuz crisis began, 10-year OAT yields have risen 150bps (US +115bps, 
Italy +132bps, Germany +83bps) as the prolonged energy crisis exacerbates fiscal concerns in 
a politically fragile environment. Franc e’ fiscal deficits have remained above 5% of GDP for 
four consecutive years. Debt has risen from 98% of GDP in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, and 
the 2026 deficit is now expected at -5.4% against a -5% initial target. 
• A "short Europe" trade amplifies the move and spreads it to the rest of the Eurozone while 
the ECB looks the other way. Foreign banks and non-banks hold 36% of OATs and Japanese 
investors about 5% (EUR137bn), exposing France to the repatriation trade and price-sensitive 
investors. Speculative shorts, hedge -fund asset -swap unwinds and thin liquidity feed a 
negative loop, w ith market depth more than 50% below normal and the OAT spread over 
Bunds peaking at 150bp s. The speed reflects a positioning shock, not a pure repricing of 
fundamentals. Long -term investors are sitting out ahead of the elections, given budget 
uncertainty and the sensitivity of OATs to political fragility. Spanish and Italian spreads are 
about 20bps wider, bank CDS are widening and French and Italian three -month bills are 
converging toward the ECB's marginal lending rate. Fragmentation is far from 2012 or 2020 
levels, but the ingredients are in place. The ECB has not yet intervened and may limit  itself to 
verbal intervention, while continuing QT and pushing through two more hikes (+50bps).  
• Baseline: A watered -down budget, but the fiscal arithmetic stays heavy for France .  
We expect GDP growth of +0.5% in 2026 and +0.9% in 2027. The Assembly votes on the budget 
on 20 and 27 October, and we expect a budget by year -end through ordinances or Article 
49.3, keeping the 2027 deficit at -5.3% of GDP. This should contain fiscal slippage and stabilize 
financial conditions, with OAT spreads in the 120 -150bps range. Higher rates  (+100bps) will 
feed through over the next three to six months, adding EUR25bn (0.8% of GDP) in gross 
interest expenses for households, non -financial firms and the government combined. The 
debt-stabilizing deficit is -2.7% of GDP, which requires savings of more than  EUR80bn, plus 
EUR15-20bn a year to stop social and interest spending from widening the deficit. This takes 
credible fiscal measures (better tax mix, higher retirement age) and growth -enhancing 
reforms. 
• Adverse scenarios: France-centered crisis or Eurozone crisis? The Middle East situation is far 
from contained; oil prices could stay above USD100/bbl for another six months , leading to a 
further global bond sell -off hitting France the hardest, tightening financial conditions and 
jeopardizing deficit reduction. Domestically, opposition parties could take down the 
government or slippage surprises resurface despite a passed budget, tightening conditions in 
the same way. In this context, GDP would stall at +0.0% in 2027 and spread s reach 150 -
175bps. The ECB would talk up and potentially launch repo intervention. In our view, it would 
take a substantial Eurozone -wide contraction (France -0.5% in 2027 ; Eurozone -0.2%; OAT 
spreads at 175 -200bps) for the ECB to tweak or stop QT, launch open market and repo 
intervention and stabilize funding markets (as OATs and BTPs make up 50% of euro repo 
collateral). 
• Looking ahead, the 2027 elections are the next test, and TPI is a late and uncertain 
backstop. With the presidential election on 18 A pril and 2 May 2027 (legislative elections 
likely to follow), we see four scenarios: i) a President with an absolute majority and allies, who 
delivers fiscal consolidation; ii) a President short of a majority who seeks alliances  (our 
baseline, with the 2028 deficit at -4.9% and spreads above 100bps) ; iii) a President short of a 
majority without alliances  and iv) a political and fiscal crisis, for instance from unfunded tax 
cuts or spending hikes, with spreads above 200bps and widespread contagion. Only in t his 
last scenario would the ECB activate the Transmission Protection Instrument (TPI). The risk is 
a lack of political will rather than of instruments, and redenomination risk is the tail case.

Toasted, not burnt: France’s bond sell-off  
and the road to the 2027 election

### Page 2

France is the country most affected in the global bond sell-off

Since the start of the Hormuz crisis,  yields on 10 -year French government bonds (OAT) have risen by 150bps  
(vs 115bps for the US, 132bps for Italy and 83bps for Germany), the highest jump among developed economies. 
The feedback loop between rising oil prices and interest rate expectations  has met structurally vulnerable public 
finances whose credibility has been increasingly undermined by growing political fragility since the 2024 dissolution 
of the National Assembly. France’s debt-to-GDP ratio has surged from 98% in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, with 
over 121% of GDP expected in 2027 (vs 83% to 87% in the Eurozone). Meanwhile, the  weakening economy (+0.5% 
GDP growth expected this year)  is leading to a fourth consecutive  year of fiscal slippage : The government now 
expects a deficit of -5.4% of GDP against the -5.1% targeted in the 2026 Budget. With Moody's upcoming review on 
23 October, France is now at risk of losing its AA rating (currently Aa3 by Moody’s vs A+ by S&P and Fitch Ratings, 
i.e. already one notch below); current pricing, if sustained, would even imply a BBB rating.

Table 1: Macroeconomic and fiscal forecasts for France in 2027

Government Allianz Research 
Real GDP growth (%) 1.0 0.9 
Inflation (CPI. %) 1.8 2.2 
Public balance (% GDP) -5.0 -5.3 
Primary balance (% GDP) -2.1 -2.5 
Debt service costs (% GDP) 2.9 2.8 
Public debt (% GDP) 121.7 121.8

Sources: French government, Allianz Research

France is the first victim of the Japanese repatriation trade.  More than other European sovereigns, France's 
investor base is exposed to price -sensitive players. The most volatile group, foreign banks and non -banks, holds 
36% of French government bonds. Domestic banks and non-banks, whose holdings fluctuate less, account for 32%, 
while non -price sensitive investors (domestic and foreign central banks) make up only 32%. Other European 
countries have either a stronger anchor in the domestic market (Italy  with 66% vs 47% for France ) or with foreign 
central banks ( Germany with 26% vs 16 % for France as Bunds as the primary FX reserve vehicle). Among foreign 
holders, Japanese investors play a significant role , holding around 5% of outstanding French government bonds 
(~EUR137bn). As interest rates rise in Japan, these positions become less attractive relative to  domestic holdings. 
At best they are no longer rolled over, at worst they are actively liquidated. In the recent bond sell -off France 
became the first victim of the J apanese repatriation trade, even though it plays only a minor role compared with 
the US as an overweight in Japanese investors' benchmark allocations .

Figure 1: France’s risk premium is very sensitive to changes in political fragility

### Page 3

Fiscal concerns, political fragility and investor base have created the vulnerability ; sentiment and positioning 
determine the market reaction. A "short Europe" trade has built up for some time in markets and France is identified 
as the most vulnerable link (Figure 2). A negative feedback loop is now at work. A bearish narrative around political 
fragility and fiscal risk is amplified by very large speculative short positions, sales from Japanese institutional 
investors, hedge fund unwinding asset swap positions and a l imited presence of buyers, with market depth more 
than 50% below normal. In such conditions , even small trades can move prices sharply. OAT yields have spiked 
rapidly, with the 10-year spread vs the Bund widening to a peak 150bps, rising twice as fast as during the Eurozone 
crisis. Realized OAT spread volatility has jumped to the 90th percentile. These market dynamics point to massive 
repositioning rather than a repricing of fundamentals . But as long as a stabilizing force of buyers is missing, this 
volatility can persist.

Figure 2: A Short Europe trade has built up with pressure on EGB spreads and EUR -USD

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

As c ontagion has switched side s, the European Government Bond (EGB) market could move from country -
specific fragility to general fragmentation. The OAT sell-off has already spilled over to other EGBs. Unlike 15 years 
ago, when France fell victim to contagion from the periphery, it is now the other way round. Spanish and Italian 
bond yields rose significantly, while German yields fell. Their 10-year spreads versus the Bund widened by around 
20bps. This decoupling within the EGB space is accompanied by widening bank CDS spreads, especially in France. 
Meanwhile, Euro funding market is under elevated stress. French and Italian three-month bills are converging 
toward the ECB's Marginal Lending Facility rate, the effective ceiling for Eurozone money-market rates. A break 
above the MLF would signal a disruption in funding  markets. The ingredients for a broader fragmentation of 
monetary conditions are thus in place, even if the current magnitude  remains far from those of  2012 or 2020. 
However, monetary transmission is also a function  of time: If these stretched conditions persist for an extended 
period, an intervention of the ECB will be more likely , e specially if we see a persistent decoupling of yield 
movements between core and periphery that can feed fears of redenomination risk.

What’s next?

The 2027 budget bill is facing stiff opposition at the National Assembly, putting the planned deficit reduction in 
jeopardy. Presented to the cabinet on 1 October, the draft budget (PLF) and the social security finance bill (PLFSS) 
for 2027 target a public deficit of -5.0% of GDP, down from -5.4% in 2026. The budget is essentially about preventing 
the deficit from getting worse rather than a fiscal adjustment. The government has announced  EUR43bn of new 
fiscal-measures (1.4% of GDP) , split between EUR25bn (58%) in spending restraint (including slowing spending 
compared to expected trends such as healthcare spending, central government cuts, social spending restraint, local 
authorities cuts) and EUR18bn (42%) in new revenues.

First, opposition parties are already pushing back against most of the big revenue raisers measures, including the

### Page 4

cut to the tax allowance on pensioners’ income (EUR1.4bn), the taxation of sick leave (0.5bn) and cutbacks on 
funding to local governments (EUR2.5bn). Second, the persistence of elevated energy prices and tighter financial 
conditions could pressure GDP growth next year, denting expected government receipts. The government expects 
GDP growth at +1% in 2027, while we e xpect +0.9% but with increasing downside risks. The next key milestones to 
watch will be 20th and 27 October when the National Assembly votes on the Draft budget part I (revenues) and 
the Social Security finance bill (Figure 3). The debt stabilizing fiscal deficit stands at -2.7% of GDP, half of today’s 
level, which entails an improvement of the public deficit of EUR83bn from 2027 on top of around EUR15-20bn of 
additional savings to find each year  just to prevent the deficit from rising  (to contain rising social security and 
interest spending).

Figure 3: France’s key political & budget milestones

Note: legislative elections are not yet scheduled – the National Assembly’s term runs to 2029 unless the next president dissolves 
it. Sources: LOLF art. 45, Constitution art. 47, Assemblée nationale budget calendar of 15 Jul 2026, rating agencies, Allianz  
Research

We lay out different scenarios of what could happen between now and the elections (Figure 4). Starting in the 
short-term, we envisage three broad possibilities: i) a watered-down budget passed before year-end which plans a 
stabilization or a slight improvement of the deficit, ii) a political crisis  with opposition parties taking down the 
government and iii) a re-intensification of the energy crisis.

Figure 4: Economic, political and market scenarios October 2026-April 2027

Source: Allianz Research 
In our baseline scenario, we  expect the French government will manage to pass a watered-down budget while 
the energy crisis eases,  allowing the deficit to remain in check in 2027 (-5.3% of GDP) Opposition parties do not

### Page 5

have an incentive  to soften their stance with the Presidential elections approaching on 18 April and 2 May 2027. 
But they also do not have an incentive to create a political crisis by taking down the government. Notably, the 
National Rally (Rassemblement National, RN) has expressed a preference for implementing a budget rather than 
relying on the ‘’Special Law’’ mechanism that was used in early 2025 and early 2026 In this environment, we expect 
the government to manage to pass a mini budget, by year -end, of limited fiscal effort just to keep the deficit in 
check (at -5.3% of GDP), either through executive ordinances – a budgetary mechanism never previously utilized 
under the Fifth Republic – or by triggering Article 49.3.  
In this baseline scenario, OAT spreads should enter a phase of stabilization but contagion risk remains. After the 
clean-up of derivative positions, we see 10 -year OAT spreads trading in a pre -election range of 120 -150bps. But 
the evolution of the French spread will not be isolated , with the potential to trigger a renewed contagion move.  
The French budget and election situation feeds the “short Europe trade”, and this negative sentiment dominates 
country-specific fundamentals. We also do not believe that stabilizing the French situation alone will be enough to 
bring the EGB market back into a convergence trade. Now that dislocations have emerged, EGB spreads are likely 
to become the new playground for speculative (leveraged) players looking to exploit them. With this comes 
elevated rates and FX  volatility and the risk of money market stress. We expect the ECB in this case to limit itself 
first to verbal intervention like a change in wording from "monitoring" to "ready to act" or "disorderly market 
dynamics". We see the tipping point at a 10-year OAT spread above 150bps. 
In a more adverse scenario where the energy crisis does not ease and/or France slips into another political crisis, 
a crisis emerges, either France-centered, or with spillovers and contagion to other Eurozone countries.  Under a 
scenario of renewed tensions in energy markets, the global markets selloff would intensify. Financial conditions 
would tighten more in France, weakening the economy and putting in jeopardy the deficit reduction. Alternatively, 
the government could be taken down in Parliament, triggering a grave political crisis and leading to tighter 
financial conditions. Even under our baseline scenario of a watered-down budget, fiscal slippages could re-emerge 
– for example if the economy is weaker than expected, or if the fiscal adjustment fall s short of what is planned  
(e.g. if revenue -raising measures disappoint). Additionally, under a Special Law, the risk of wider deficit could 
increase if continued through the presidential elections  in 2027, which could lead to France -centered crisis or a 
Eurozone crisis if tightening financial conditions in France spill over to other Eurozone countries . In a France -
centered crisis we would expect the French economy t o enter a mid-sized recession as tighter financial conditions 
would weigh on domestic demand (see next section) , with GDP at +0.0% in 2027. In a Eurozone crisis , we would 
expect French GDP to contract -0.5% and the whole Eurozone economy -0.2%.  
In this adverse scenario, we could see the 10-year OAT spread rising to 1 75-200bps. This would create 
widespread contagion in the EGB market and in the banking sector. In this case, it will clearly be up to the ECB not 
to let the situation escalate into a euro crisi s 2.0. Unlike in the euro crisis, when the escalation was fueled by a lack 
of instruments while the political will was there, this time the deployment of the instruments (TPI, OMT) could fail 
due to a lack of political will and institutional inertia. It wo uld be particularly precarious if markets began to price 
redenomination risk, i.e. the possibility of a country leaving the Eurozone. But we see this scenario as a tail risk. If 
the EGB and money markets move towards general fragmentation, we expect the ECB to set operational steps (QT 
tweak, temporary operations) at OAT spreads around 175bps, but only alongside broader contagion with BTP 
spreads around 150bp s and Spain and Portugal widening together. For France, a QT tweak would represent a 
relief of EUR4.8bn on average per month, equivalent to around 20% of monthly issuances since June. But the 
problem is that “Europe short traders” can also do this math and adjust accordingly. At this point the ECB would 
not only operate in the secondary bond market but a lso need to stabilize funding markets as in  French OAT and 
Italian BTP represent 50% of the EUR repo market collateral. The TPI activation would be late in the process and 
require OAT spread around 200bps with widespread and persistent contagion.

In 2027, the presidential (and likely legislative) elections  could re -escalate economic and market pressures.  
We envisage four scenarios, which we detail more in Figure 5. In a nutshell, under our baseline assumptions, the

### Page 6

newly elected President will not secure a n absolute majority when she or he provokes legislative elections.  
Nevertheless, the President will have the strongest group at the National Assembly and will benefit from strong 
popular support. In this environment, we would expect the President to negotiate with some opposition parties to 
pass a budget putting the deficit on a downward trajectory. Nevertheless, deficit reduction will be limited ( -4.9% 
GDP deficit expected in 2028) amid strong aversion for rapid fiscal tightening in Parliament and public opinion. The 
10-year spread will likely remain above 100bps. In an adverse scenario of political and fiscal crisis, for instance 
triggered by the enactment of tax cuts and/or spending hikes  not credibly funded, a France-centered or Eurozone 
crisis scenario would re-emerge. In the latter case, the ECB would likely intervene with the TPI with French spreads 
breaking 200bps and Italian spreads above 175bps.

Figure 5:  Economic, political and market scenarios after April 2027

Source: Allianz Research

In the short term, a significant easing of global yields and elevated French spreads hinges on three factors:   
1) a substantial easing of the energy crisis, which would reduce inflationary pressures, limiting or even ending 
further central bank hikes. 2) Strong and credible fiscal -consolidation measures passed by decree, though this 
remains unlikely before the presidential election. One option used previously is  reducing healthcare spending, but 
the amount of savings is limited to EUR5-10bn (Table 2). 3) A cooling US economy could moderate the global bond 
sell off, though this too seems less likely before 2027.

Table 2: Measures the French government can implement by decrees to bypass parliamentary vote

Measure Can government act without new parliamentary vote? 
Cancel already authorized State spending Yes 
Freeze/cancel some budgetary appropriations Yes 
Adjust medicine reimbursement within regulatory powers Yes 
Adjust healthcare tariffs/payment parameters Yes, within statutory framework 
Tighten implementation of existing social schemes Often yes 
Change parameters of social contribution rebates  Sometimes yes 
Abolish/reduce a statutory benefit Generally, no 
Permanently change tax rates No 
Fundamentally redesign social-contribution exemptions Generally, requires legislation 
Change the ONDAM target itself Generally, Parliament/LFSS

Sources: French government, various, Allianz Research 
In the longer-term, only a sizeable fiscal -consolidation program can ease French spreads sustainably.  
Ideally, a combination of the following would have the most benefits:

### Page 7

Credible fiscal adjustment (the least growth -hurting). On the revenue side, fiscal consolidation should rely on a 
more efficient tax mix to limit negative growth effects. An increase of  VAT rates would bring large receipts without 
hurting potential growth. Freez ing income-tax thresholds should be preferred to increase social contributions to 
limit the negative impact on labor demand and labor supply amid already high labor taxation in France. Some 
inefficient and costly tax rebates and loopholes could also be cut down. But most of the fiscal adjustment should 
be operated through expenditure cuts. An increase of the effective age of retirement – for instance through a rise 
in the number of years of contribution – would bring substantial savings and increase tax receipts. Substantial 
savings could also be enacted on public agencies and local governments’ administrative costs. In parallel, the 
government could launch a comprehensive spending review to improve efficiency  of public expenditure, 
benchmarking what has been done in the past in countries such as Canada and Sweden.  
Growth-enhancing reforms. The government could implement structural reforms which will likely support growth 
in the medium -term, reducing the need for further painful fiscal adjustment later on. A pension reform would not 
only bring higher receipts and increase savings; it would also stimulate labor supply and potential growth 1. Other 
growth-enhancing measures include a revamp of school education met hods, scaling back excessive regulation, 
incentivizing higher-skilled education and hiring through a reform of the taxation system.

The trickle-down effect of tighter financial conditions

The ongoing bond market problem could increasingly become an economic problem through tighter financial 
and credit conditions, but the economy has buffers to sustain a short -term financial shock. Higher sovereign 
interest rates have started to filter through on to consumer, mortgage and corporate funding costs, and the pa ss-
through will continue through 2027. This will tighten the credit and financial cycle, through higher borrowing costs 
on new lending, higher refinancing bills and tighter access to credit, and ultimately weaken consumer and 
investment spending. Our BIS-built Financial condition index (FCI) for France shows that overall financial conditions 
have tightened sharply recently, but from a relatively loose level (Figure 6, left). They are now close to their long -
term historical averages. Besides, the credit impu lse for households and corporations remain s supportive to date 
(Figure 6, right). During the Eurozone crisis of 2011 -13, the credit impulse turned sharply negative but GDP growth 
held up. While the latest ECB Bank Lending Survey (BLS) points to the credit impulses weakening in the next couple 
of quarters, this does not necessarily mean that GDP growth will weaken sharply. The French economy has buffers 
to withstand a negative finance shock, provided it does not last too long and does not get worse. In parti cular, 
households have high savings and corporates’ cash reserves have been rebuilt recently, though many firms struggle 
with elevated bankruptcies.

Figure 6: France’s financial conditions index (left); credit impulses & GDP growth (right)

Sources: LSEG Datastream, BIS, Allianz Research 
Note: last data point in the FCI is 5th October 2026.

French households, non -financial corporates and the government. We find that  a +100bps increase in interest

rates would almost double households’ net interest income in 2026 to -EUR16.3bn from -EUR8.5bn in 2025, or the

Figure 7: Impact of a +100bps shock on interest rates for French households

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

For French non-financial companies, around 50.6% of debt is due to reset or mature within the next 12 months, 
well below the level in Italy, Spain, the UK and the US, where 78 to 90% is due for refinancing. However, France 
starts from the largest corporate debt stock in Europe in this sample, at around EUR2.27trn. This means that even 
with a lower refinancing share, around EUR1.15trn of debt still needs to be repriced within a year, more than in 
Germany, It aly o r Spain. In addition, the pressure is amplified by the starting level of borrowing costs. French 
corporates already face an effective interest rate of 6.1%, the highest among the European peers shown here. Under 
a +100bp s interest-rate shock, the effective pass -through would amount to roughly +89bp s, adding around 
EUR10bn to annual interest expenses. Relative to gross operating surplus, this represents an additional burden of 
about 2.1% of GOS, the highest impact across similar peers, and could shave roughl y -0.7pp off gross margins (see 
Figure 6). For the French government, a +100bps increase in the weighted average interest rates would mean +0.3% 
of GDP in 2027 (EUR 11.7bn) and +0.7% of GDP by 2035. Indeed, France has a rather long average maturity of its 
debt (>8.5 years) which protects it from very high volatility on its interest expenses.

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Any forecast rests on assumptions. Each might be
individually reasonable, but if they all tilt in the same
direction, they look less plausible in combination.
• Different assumptions can also drive different conclusions,
as can be seen in Treasury and RBA views on trend growth

in supply capacity. Treasury’s long-term view on both
productivity growth and labour force participation trends is
systematically less downbeat than the RBA’s (shorter-term)
view. Treasury’s estimate of the stable-inflation rate of
unemployment is also lower than the RBA’s estimate.
• We consider the risks around the RBA’s view to be
asymmetric. The RBA’s assumptions implicitly require a
repeat of the factors that dragged recent productivity
outcomes down, despite strong global investment, policy

changes domestically and the possible benefits of AI. Some
observers appear to extrapolate this weakness beyond the
RBA’s forecast horizon. But this would be hard to square
with the reasonable belief that the global interest rate
structure will be higher in future than it was pre-pandemic.
A systematic tilt, even if unintentional and understandable,
may point to the likely direction of future surprises.
Any forecast rests on assumptions, and the further out

you forecast, the harder it is to ground those assumptions
in something real. Add the uncertainty about AI, where
predictions range from “unbounded prosperity without
needing to work” to “we will all be dead in a couple of years”,
and you can see why assuming the future looks like the past is
an attractive fall-back position.
How much of the past, though? This is the context for the
debate about the Intergenerational Review (IGR)  released this
week. In one corner, Treasury assumes that future productivity

growth will average 1.2%yr over the next 40 years, in line with
the 30-year average, and that labour force participation will
continue to trend up, as it has for the past five decades, until
2040. In the other corner, the RBA assumes that productivity
growth will average 0.7%yr, in line with the past 20 years
– essentially the post-GFC and pandemic period – and that
participation rates will be flat to down in the near term.
Both productivity assumptions have limitations: round-number-

of-year averages might be distorted by capturing a downturn
without the matching upturn, or vice versa. Peak-to-peak or
trough-to-trough is always preferable. Even that calculation
cannot escape the weakness of recent years, though.
When your assumptions have only upside risk
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
To be fair, the RBA’s productivity assumptions relate only to
the short term and are not strictly comparable to the IGR,
as the Governor has emphasised this week.  By contrast, the

near-term divergence in forecasts for the participation rate,
which my colleague Ryan Wells  first highlighted in May , is a
genuine divergence.
The RBA’s more downbeat set of assumptions could be
unwittingly contributing to a self-fulfilling talking-down
of Australia’s prospects that clearly appeals to some
commentators outside the public sector. Many in this group
view productivity growth as stemming wholly or mostly from
policy “reforms”, harking back to the halcyon Hawke–Keating

era of deregulation. Proponents point to the lack of reform
recently to justify this downbeat view of growth capacity. We
think this misses the global nature of that slowdown.
Part of the trouble with the downbeat view of future
productivity is that it implicitly assumes that the factors
weighing on productivity growth in the past will continue.
But there are good reasons not to expect the same post-GFC
weakness in risk appetite, and so investment and capital

accumulation, decades later and absent another crisis. The AI
boom and associated resurgence of risk appetite make it hard
to argue that we will see the same lack of new productivity-
enhancing technologies and general malaise as that period.
For Australia specifically, assuming the same low average
productivity growth as the past 20 years would require
assuming a compositional drag s imilar to a repeat of the
run-up of the care economy and decline in measured mining

production over the past 15 years. To be fair, decarbonisation
does imply further declines in (capital-intensive, high-
productivity) coal production, dragging mining productivity
down further. It would be a stretch, though, to assume
expansion in critical and transition minerals provides no offset.
We can grant that AI’s impact on productivity will take a while
to come through and still expect some boost in coming years
– at least by the end of the RBA’s forecast horizon in 2028.

Indeed, some observers already see an AI-induced boost to
service-sector activity in the recent upside surprise in UK GDP.
Some productivity pessimists might want to argue that the
“RBA assumptions collectively imply a
downbeat view of supply growth."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
rest of the world will benefit from the AI boom, but somehow
not Australia, or not until later. Such an extraordinary claim
requires a mechanism explaining that divergence, though.
The Productivity Commission estimated back in December
that AI could boost multifactor productivity by 2.3%pt over

the course of the next decade, or roughly a quarter-point per
year extra growth on average – a big difference in the scheme
of usual growth rates in output and productivity. Labour
productivity growth would be boosted a little more; the data
centre boom is expanding the capital stock. But an estimate
published in December is unlikely to adequately capture the
step change in capability of the main models, especially the
coding and agentic harnesses, that emerged over the summer

of 2025/26. Given what we understand to be limited adoption
of AI in the Australian public sector, policymakers might also
be systematically underestimating both the technology’s
capabilities and the breadth of uptake in the private sector.
As an illustration of how downbeat you need to be to
extrapolate the RBA’s near-term assumption further out,
suppose that all AI does is make about 10% of the economy
10% more productive over four years. This extra 1% of

economy-wide productivity is well below most estimates
of the impact. It would nonetheless add a quarter-point on
annual productivity growth for four years, bringing a downbeat
0.7% assumption up to almost 1%.
A deeper challenge is that, beyond the RBA’s near-term
horizon, low productivity growth is hard to square with a
higher future average for interest rates globally.
We note, for example, recent comments by RBA Deputy
Governor Hauser, pointing to 700 years of bond yield data

to suggest that the post-GFC period of low interest rates
was an anomaly, something we noted two years ago. The
global structure of interest rates emerges to balance global
saving and investment, and stronger investment for AI and
other purposes is clearly in evidence. We find it implausible
to assume that all that investment happens, pushing up
global yields, and none of it delivers higher productivity. Even
increased defence spending has historically had productivity

payoffs, as Ukraine’s drone manufacturing ecosystem has
exemplified recently.
It is understandable that when inflation is above target, a
central bank would rather err on the side of caution than
techno-optimism. They know that being downbeat, and
running policy a little too tight, means a faster return of
inflation to target, which is no bad thing for an inflation-
targeting central bank.
It matters, though, when those understandable assumptions

are systematically one-sided. If there are arguments that
productivity growth over the next few years will be even lower
than the 0.7%yr 20-year average, we are yet to see them. In
contrast, there are reasonable arguments for it to be a bit higher.
Similarly, the RBA’s participation rate assumptions are low
given near-term demographic trends , and out of step with
Treasury’s revised projections. Today’s labour market data
for August shows the participation rate rising again, to just

12½ basis points below the January 2025 all-time high. This
supports the idea that RBA’s view on participation trends
is too downbeat. The IGR’s NAIRU (stable-inflation rate of
unemployment) assumption is a quarter-point or so below
RBA estimates revealed in various FOI requests and the
Governor’s comments this week.
Together, the three RBA assumptions imply weaker growth
in supply capacity and thus more inflationary pressure for any
given level of demand. If every one of your assumptions seems

individually plausible, but all skew in the one direction, it
starts to look like a pattern. The thumb on the scale is unlikely
to be intentional, but it does suggest the likely direction of
future surprises in the medium term.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: the importance of supply
This week, market participants have focused on discussions
between the US and China on trade, security and technology
as President Xi visits Washington.
Preliminary discussions between US Treasury Secretary

Bessent and Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng ahead of
President Xi’s arrival in Washington were characterised by
both sides as productive, and Treasury Secretary Bessent
subsequently announced that discussions in Washington
would leave trade negotiations for upcoming meetings
between the two leaders in Shenzhen and Miami. To allow
room for this deferral, the current trade truce will be extended
until January 2027. President Trump subsequently outlined

that discussions during President Xi’s Washington visit would
instead focus on security, technology and AI.
A more constructive tone was also seen between the US and
Iran overnight, if press reports are accurate. According to
Bloomberg and Reuters, sources suggest the US and Iran are
again exploring a staged deal which would see Iran re-open
the Strait of Hormuz and the US lift its blockade of Iranian
ports. Negotiations are occurring on the sidelines of the UN

General Assembly, with Qatari officials mediating.
President Trump continues to show little interest in a deal
before November’s mid-term elections, but the global
economy is under increasing pressure as the price of Brent oil
hovers between USD100 and USD110 while refining spreads
add additional impetus to refined product prices. The mooted
consideration of a potential US diesel export ban this week
makes clear that the US is not immune from this pressure

despite having significant domestic energy supply and refining
capacity, relative to their own demand.
The consequences of the current energy supply shock and other
disruptions the US have experienced over the past year remain
front of mind for FOMC members. Those speaking this week such
as NY and Cleveland Fed Presidents Williams and Hammack
highlighted the risk to inflation expectations from a series of
supply shocks when the economy is fully employed, and the

likelihood of an additional hike(s) being required to rein in current
inflation momentum and risks. Williams noted that expectations
of another hike by year end are "reasonable" and that the FOMC
have "a lot of work to do". Debt market’s concern remains acute,
having now almost priced in four hikes over the coming year and
taken the US 10-year above 5.00%, currently 5.20%.
Australia’s only data release this week was the August
Labour Force Survey . Employment surprised to the upside,

rising 39.5k. But, once again, new labour demand was more
than offset by supply as the participation rate bounced
0.2ppts higher to 67.1%, just shy of January 2025’s 115-year
high of 67.2%. As a result, the unemployment rate increased
from 4.5% to 4.6% and was close to rounding up to 4.7%
(4.65% to two decimal places).
There may be some residual seasonality in August’s print
related to the new survey collection model. However, the
underlying trend is still consistent with a gradual easing in the

labour market, seeing the unemployment rate drift higher. We
expect this to continue through to the first half of 2027.
The strength in labour force participation also speaks to the
broader issue of Australia’s supply capacity. As discussed
by Chief Economist Luci Ellis , the RBA’s assumptions around
participation, productivity and the NAIRU collectively
skew towards a weak assessment of supply capacity. For
participation in particular, the RBA’s near-term assumptions

are downbeat compared to recent demographic trends and
also sit below Treasury’s longer-term projections as set out in
the 2026 IGR. As above, the August LFS emphasises that slack
could emerge more quickly than the RBA’s forecasts imply
without an outright fall in employment.
Ahead of next week’s RBA meeting and inflation data,
our latest state-level analysis  provides perspective on the
persistence of underlying price pressures, particularly the

importance of housing-related components. For a broader
assessment of the fiscal backdrop and the implications for the
states, our latest state fiscal update  explores how the housing
market correction and changes to NDIS funding arrangements
are pressuring state finances.
Before turning to this week’s events offshore, it is important
to highlight that last Friday the Bank of Japan raised its
policy rate by 25bp to 1.25%. Policymakers maintained a

constructive assessment of the economy, pointing to resilient
consumption, ongoing labour market tightness, and solid
investment spending. More importantly, the Bank expressed
growing confidence that inflation is increasingly being
sustained by domestic wage and demand dynamics rather
than imported cost shocks alone.
In our view, the emphasis placed on improving underlying
inflation dynamics suggests policymakers are becoming
increasingly comfortable that inflation is able to be sustained by

domestic demand and wage growth. Normalisation is likely to
remain gradual, but the Bank appears firmly on a path towards
further tightening, with rates likely to reach 1.5% by mid-2027.
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

### Page 5

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Along for the ride
There was plenty of action in the markets this week, as global
interest rates continued to move higher, and New Zealand
was taken along for the ride. In the US, strong economic data
and resurgent oil prices have led the market to believe that

the central bank will need to get on with the job and hike
its policy rate further in the months ahead. Longer-term US
interest rates have continued to lift sharply, driving both a rise
in domestic interest rates and a fall in the New Zealand dollar,
with the trade-weighted index already 4% below what the
RBNZ assumed in its September Monetary Policy Statement.
While the September MPS worked to dampen market
expectations about the pace of future rate hikes, offshore

developments have seen market pricing for the OCR bounce back
and exceed previous levels. Interest rate markets are currently
pricing around a 75% chance of a hike at the 28 October policy
review, with a high chance of another hike on top of this at the 9
December review. We’d question whether the RBNZ’s thinking
has really shifted to that degree in the last few weeks though.
Notably, the RBNZ provided some unscheduled comments on
the monetary policy outlook this week, in what was otherwise

a very light local news week. In connection with a visit to
Dunedin by Governor Breman and other RBNZ leaders, the
RBNZ put out a media release that included the following:
“If higher oil prices persist, they are expected to result in
somewhat higher near-term inflation than we assumed in the
September Statement… As always, we will assess incoming
data and global developments ahead of our next decision in
October and remain focused on the outlook for inflation over

the medium term.”
Both of these statements are fairly benign and factual in
themselves. What’s more notable is that the RBNZ felt the
need to make a public comment at all; the occasion didn’t
require one. (The Governor’s presentation at a business lunch
was originally billed as being based on material from the
September MPS, with no new insights.)
If the intention was to guide market expectations for the
October meeting lower, it wasn’t entirely successful. Indeed,

the market initially fixated on the first part of the statement –
higher near-term inflation – and pushed pricing for an October
hike even higher on the day. While this was unwound the
following day, market pricing for OCR hikes remains higher
than it was at the start of the week.
As it stands, that leaves the RBNZ with the prospect of
rattling the market if it follows through with the October
pause that it signalled in September MPS. However, it’s

possible that the local and offshore economic data, together
with geopolitical developments, could shift market pricing in
the meantime. Domestically, the NZIER’s quarterly survey of
business opinion (6 Oct) and the September quarter CPI (22
Oct) will be especially important in that regard.
Turning to the data, the only release this week was our
Employment Confidence Index, produced in conjunction with
McDermott Miller. Compared to our consumer confidence

survey, there was a relatively weak bounce in employment
confidence in the September quarter. The index rose by 3.2
points to 86.3, leaving it at the second-lowest level on record
for this survey going back to 2004.
Perceptions of current job opportunities were unchanged at a
net -60%. This is the component of the survey that seems to
have the most predictive power for near-term labour market
conditions and is broadly consistent with the unemployment

rate holding at its current 11-year high of 5.6%. Hiring has
remained soft to an unusual degree for this stage of the
economic cycle, and those who are out of the workforce have
been finding it increasingly difficult to get in.
It’s clear that there is still substantial slack in the labour
market and little upward pressure on wages, which would
push back somewhat against the market’s view that the RBNZ
will need to pick up the pace and deliver two more OCR hikes

before Christmas. The RBNZ doesn’t need to have employment
formally in its mandate for it to be attentive to the signals
coming from the labour market.
The data flow picks up again next week, with monthly releases
for employment, building activity, and business and consumer
confidence – see our previews on the following page. The
other release of note is the Pre-Election Fiscal and Economic
Update (PREFU) on Tuesday. This ‘book opening’ exercise
is designed to create a level playing field, allowing parties

outside the current Government and the public more generally
to judge to the state of the public finances in the leadup to the
General Election on 7 November. In the case of the opposition
parties, it will also allow them to calibrate the spending and
taxation policies on which they will campaign. See our preview
for the details on what we expect from this release.
Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Aug Household Spending Indicator (%mth)
Sep 29, Last: 1.1%, WBC f/c: –0.5%
Mkt f/c: 0.3%, Range: –0.5% to 0.7%
The household spending indicator rose 1.1%mth in July,
comfortably exceeding expectations and extending the strong
momentum evident through the middle of the year. Annual

growth accelerated to 7.0%yr, the fastest pace since early
2023.
Our Westpac-DataX Card Tracker points to softer spending
conditions through August and early September, with higher
fuel prices increasingly driving spending growth and, at the
margin, squeezing spending elsewhere. The detail suggests
a modest rotation away from discretionary categories, with
hospitality spending easing and vehicle sales also likely
moderating after a strong June quarter. Together with a softer

read from our MHSI-aligned card measure, we anticipate the
household spending indicator will decline 0.5%mth in August.
AUS: RBA Policy Decision (%)
Sep 29, Last: 4.35, Westpac f/c: 4.60
Market f/c: 4.60, Range: 4.60
Westpac anticipates the RBA Monetary Policy Board will raise
the cash rate by 25bps to 4.60% at its September meeting.
As discussed by Chief Economist Luci Ellis recent rhetoric from
the RBA have escalated, particularly with Governor Bullock

flagging that upside inflation risks appear to be "materialising"
– the conditionality set in August for further rate hikes.
There is a risk of a follow-up hike, though it crucially depends
on the data flow after the meeting. For September, we
continue to expect a split vote at the meeting, with material
differences in opinion about trend growth in supply capacity,
as discussed on Page 2.
AUS: Aug Headline CPI (%mth)
Sep 30, Last: 1.0, Westpac f/c: 0.4

Market f/c: 0.5, Range: 0.4 to 0.9
The July CPI reversed May and June weakness with a larger
than expected 1.0% increase. This was stronger than
Westpac’s expectation of 0.8% and market’s expectation of
0.9%. The annual pace eased to 3.5%yr from 3.8%yr.
We are looking for a 0.4% increase in the August CPI. Food &
non-alcoholic beverages to rise 0.2%mth with solid gains for
bread & cereal products (0.4%), meat & seafood (0.5%) and
food products n.e.c. (0.4%) partially offset by falling non-

alcoholic beverages (–0.6%) and a soft print for meals out &
take away food (0.1%).
At two decimal points it is a 0.43% increase in August so the
risk this month is to the upside.
For more details please see “ August CPI Preview ”.

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Aug Trimmed Mean CPI (%mth)
Sep 30, Last: 0.5, Westpac f/c: 0.2
Market f/c: 0.3, Range: 0.2 to 0.4
The Trimmed Mean (TM) lifted 0.5%mth/3.5%yr, on par with
last July which was the strongest monthly read in the series’
short history. The result was stronger than both our and the

market’s expectation of 0.4%mth.
August TM is expected to rise 0.2%mth lifting the annual pace
to 3.6%yr. For market services excluding volatiles and holiday
travel we have pencilled in a step down to 0.1%mth, this series
lifted 0.8% in July possibly due to the larger-than-expected
increase in minimum wages.
The distribution of the August TM is from a low of –0.32%mth
to a high of 0.53%mth. Remembering the TM is seasonally
adjusted, with rents (+0.17%mth) and dwellings (+0.20%mth)

mostly locking in the TM. See “ August CPI Preview ”.
AUS: Aug Dwelling Approvals (%mth)
Sep 30, Last: –3.6, Westpac f/c: –5.0
Market f/c: –1.0, Range: –6.0 to 1.0
Dwelling approvals fell 3.6%mth in July, with annual growth
easing to 9.0%yr. The decline was driven primarily by detached
dwellings, although unit approvals also edged lower. The
result suggests higher interest rates may be starting to weigh
on new housing demand.
We expect a similar story to play out in August, with

elevated interest rates continuing to weigh on detached
dwelling approvals, while the unit segment holds broadly
steady. Overall, dwelling approvals are expected to decline
5%mth. While risks remain tilted to the downside, residential
construction should remain relatively resilient through 2026,
supported by the strong lift in approvals through the first half
of the year and a sizeable pipeline of work yet to be done.
AUS: Aug Private Sector Credit (%mth)

Sep 30, Last: 0.6, Westpac f/c: 0.5
Market f/c: 0.5, Range: 0.3 to 0.6
Growth in private sector credit growth appeared unsustainable
over May-Jun, especially given the slowing in the housing
market. Come July, the pace now dropped from 0.8%mth to
0.6%mth. Housing credit the main source of weakness, with
investor credit growth beginning to adjust lower in response to
higher interest rates, lower house prices and, most importantly,
tax changes for investment properties. Meanwhile, business

borrowing continued to rise rapidly.
For August, we have pencilled in a 0.5%mth increase, and we
look for continued divergence between housing and business
segments.

### Page 8

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.8
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Sep Cotality Home Value Index (%mth)
Oct 1, Last: –0.9, Westpac f/c: –0.9
The Cotality home value index – covering all of Australia – fell
0.9% in August following downwardly-revised 1.2% and 0.9%
declines in July and June. Annual growth slowed to 2.7%yr.

Prices nationally are now 3.6% below their peak in March.
The September update is set to see more of the same with
the daily measure (covering the five major capital cities rather
than all of Australia) tracking a 1.2% decline in line with
this measure’s August move. Annual growth looks like to
stall about flat. The detail suggests monthly declines have
accelerated in Brisbane, Perth and Adelaide but moderated a
touch in Melbourne, noting that there is a seasonal element

to price moves in Sep. Markets are still adjusting to higher
interest rates and the tax policy changes introduced in the May
Federal budget with more weakness likely into year-end.
AUS: Aug Goods Trade Balance ($bn)
Oct 1, Last: 1.9, Westpac f/c: 2.3
Market f/c: 2.2, Range: 1.5 to 2.5
The goods trade surplus narrowed slightly to $1.9bn in July
but remained above our and consensus expectations. This
was down from an upwardly revised surplus of $2.3bn in June.

Underneath the modest headline change was considerable
volatility among components, especially in flows of non-
monetary gold, remain the chief culprit.
For August, we have pencilled in a slight widening of the trade
surplus to $2.3bn. Port data points to a bounce-back in coal
and iron ore export volumes,  and prices looked to have edged
higher. However, volatility in non-monetary gold trade and
other hard-to-predict sub-categories (like ADP equipment)

remain a wildcard.
NZ: Aug Monthly Employment Indicator (%mth)
Sep 28, Last: 0.3, Westpac f/c: 0.3
The MEI has continued to tick higher in recent months,
showing that the jobs market has held its ground through
the Iran conflict and the consequent surge in fuel prices. The
weekly snapshots provided by Stats NZ have been running
ahead of year-ago levels and suggest another solid result
for August – we’ve pencilled in a 0.3% increase, following
a similar rise in July. However, the history of this indicator

suggests that both months are likely to be revised down a bit
from their initial release.

### Page 9

Nonfarm payrolls surprised to the upside in August, 
registering a gain of 162k with 55k in back revisions to June 
and July. The report left the 3-month average at 71k, near the 
top of the estimated range consitent with balance between 
labour demand and supply. Come September, the trend is 
expected to remain in place with a 80k gain forecast. With 
inflation risks paramount, upward revisions will have a greater 
impact on market variables than a negative revision.
The decline in the participation rate over the past year has 
capped the unemployment rate. Participation is likely to edge 
higher in September and take the unemployment rate up 
to 4.2% with it. There is little available slack in the labour 
market, but plenty just outside if demand lifts.

## Document 188: Business Checkpoint_ Bigger and better Business conditions improved across small and mid-sized firms in August, with small businesses leading in profitability growth. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Business Checkpoint_ Bigger and better Business conditions improved across small and mid-sized firms in August, with small businesses leading in profitability growth. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-09-01
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13020514     1

Economy 
Business Checkpoint: Bigger and better 
24 September 2026

Key takeaways 
• Bank of America Institute's Business Checkpoint is a new evolution of our Small Business Checkpoint, broadening the data set 
from firms with <$5M in annual revenue to those with <$50M. Not only does this increase the number of businesses in our 
dataset, but mid-sized firms generate more than double the share of revenue of smaller firms, according to Census Bureau data. 
• Since May, small business profitability growth has exceeded that of mid-sized firms, with hiring-related payments also 
improving, while mid-sized firms have shown more modest gains across both measures in August, according to Bank of America 
business account data. 
• Meanwhile, payroll growth in August has strengthened among mid-sized firms despite weaker employment gains, while both 
small and mid-sized businesses have exhibited accelerated AI-related spending growth, suggesting firms are adapting to the 
current environment in different ways depending on their size.

Business Checkpoint is a new, regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It provides a real-time view of the financial health, opportunities 
and challenges facing small and mid-sized businesses across the US economy, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America's proprietary 
data. By analyzing activity among both small and mid-sized firms, Business Checkpoint offers timely insights into business profitability, hiring, 
investment and spending trends. Such data is not intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, 
financial condition or performance of Bank of America. 
Not just small anymore: Expanding the data pool 
Business Checkpoint is designed to provide a broader view of the forces shaping business performance in the US economy. 
While our previous Small Business Checkpoint focused on firms with less than $5 million in annual revenue (i.e. small firms), this 
expanded analysis also incorporates firms with annual revenues between $5 million and $50 million (i.e. mid-sized). Together, 
these businesses offer a more complete picture of profitability, hiring and investment trends across Main Street.

those enterprises with <$50M in annual revenue, according to Census Bureau data (Exhibit 2). This is more than twice as much

Does more revenue mean higher profits? 
A recent defining trend for small firms has been the recovery in profitability growth over the summer following a period in which 
firms faced a succession of pressures, including weather-related disruptions and tariffs (read more on this in the April Small 
Business Checkpoint). Does this trend apply to larger companies? 
Using Bank of America business account data, the profitability ratio for both firm sizes was above their 2025 average in August 
(Exhibit 3). For mid-sized firms, we find profitability growth has also improved in the last few months but remained below small 
firms’ growth in August (Exhibit 4).  
The reversal is notable because small businesses spent much of the past year under greater pressure, and their recent 
outperformance suggests some of the most acute headwinds facing smaller firms may be beginning to ease. Although, actual 
sales worsened in August, according to the September National Federation of Independent Business (NFIB) report. This was 
down from July and the lowest reading since November 2025.

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Tariff refunds may offset only part of rising cost pressures 
One tailwind for firms’ cash buffers is the ongoing tariff refund dispersal. According to Bank of America account data, around 
44% of estimated IEEPA-related (International Emergency Economic Powers Act) tariff payment amounts have been refunded to 
firms with <$50M in annual revenue as of August (Exhibit 5). This is up from around 5% in May, suggesting tariff refunds are 
reaching firms relatively quickly and potentially providing a near-term boost to cash buffers for some businesses. 
However, these refunds may provide only temporary relief if input costs continue to rise faster than revenues. The August 
Producer Price Index (PPI) data indicated that producer prices are increasing faster year-over-year (YoY) than consumer prices, 
suggesting firms remain exposed to ongoing margin pressure.  
The divergence is especially evident in energy-related inputs, with diesel prices up nearly 80% YoY in August (Exhibit 6). As a 
result, firms within agriculture, transportation or construction may face prolonged cost pressures (read more on rising fuel costs 
impacting small business margins in the June Small Business Checkpoint).

The labor market looks to be broadening 
Business profitability and hiring often move together, but not always at the same pace. As firms navigate slower growth and 
elevated operating costs, both official employment data and Bank of America payment flows offer insight into whether firms are 
expanding their workforce, maintaining staffing levels, or becoming more cautious about future hiring. 
Bank of America payroll payments per business client show a significant improvement at these firms compared with a year ago, 
with small business payroll growth still positive but not as strong (Exhibit 7). Yet, according to Automated Data Processing (ADP), 
firms with 50-499 employees have lagged with adding headcount over the course of this year compared to last. 
If these firms have added fewer jobs, why has their payroll growth accelerated? There are a few reasons for this, as payroll 
growth reflects more than headcount. For one, payroll growth could reflect providing larger raises to existing workers, rather 
than adding workers. This could also be why Bank of America internal deposit data shows workers across the income spectrum, 
especially in lower-income households, have seen notable wage growth recently (read more on this in the August Institute 
Employment Report).

Small businesses outpace mid-sized firms’ hiring momentum 
A firm could keep employment unchanged, increase overtime or raise average weekly hours and still generate faster payroll 
growth (read more on weekly-paid workers’ wage gains from job switching in September’s Switching 9 to 5s). This often 
happens when firms are uncertain about demand and, instead of hiring new workers, they ask existing workers to work more.  
In fact, looking at Bank of America business payments to hiring firms, small business hiring activity has improved in recent 
months, while hiring-related payments among mid-sized firms just recently started to improve in July (Exhibit 8). 
According to the most recent NFIB survey, concerns about labor quality and availability and labor costs eased in August, with the 
share of owners citing labor costs as their single most important problem falling to its lowest level since March 2021. 
Exhibit 8: Small business payments to hiring firms have accelerated since March 2026, whereas mid-sized picked up in the last two months 
Business payments to hiring firms per client by annual revenue (YoY%, monthly, three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
AI-related spending growth is ramping up across firms 
In addition to ramping up hiring, AI-related spending growth (see Methodology) among mid-sized firms has recently exceeded 
that of small businesses – 20.1% YoY compared to 18.4% YoY in August – suggesting firms may be moving quickly this year to 
adopt productivity-enhancing technologies (Exhibit 9). For businesses operating with lean staffing models and facing continued 
cost pressures, AI tools may provide a relatively inexpensive way to automate routines, expand capabilities and support growth.
